Here are the key insights:\n\n## Fixations and Saccades in Reading\n\nIn reading research, fixations correspond to the period of uptake of visual information (text), and saccades are movements of the eyes that bring other parts of the text onto the most sensitive part of the retina for information uptake. The average saccade size is 7-9 letter spaces, although this will depend on the size of the text and font type.\n\n## Mobile Eye Tracking Challenges\n\nWhile new methods such as mobile eye tracking and eye tracking in virtual/augmented realities are emerging quickly, the eye-movement terminology has scarcely been revised. A key challenge is that the same computational definitions of a fixation will not suffice for eye-tracker signals from different frames of reference: a velocity peak in the gaze coordinate signal from a mobile eye tracker need not reflect the same movement of the eye as a velocity peak in the gaze coordinate signal of a remote eye tracker.\n\n## Sampling Rate Importance\n\nAny study that aims to explore the detailed mechanics of reading needs to use an eye tracker with a sampling rate that is high enough to capture saccades, because saccades can be completed in as little as 20 ms, which requires a sampling rate of at least 100 Hz. A 120 Hz mobile eye tracker showed a significant increase in the detection rate of saccades and an improved estimation of the mean saccade duration, compared to a 60 Hz eye tracker.\n\n## Mobile Screen Scrolling Challenges\n\nWhile existing approaches work well for static stimuli, the detection of fixations and saccades for dynamic stimuli is barely better than chance. When eyes follow moving targets like a vertically scrolling mobile phone feed, a fixation is typically defined as >60 ms consecutive gaze points within an area of interest.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"4d5c6ae2-6977-4896-a481-9940127582e3","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:02:59.537Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"61d0221b-44bb-4739-bf2a-e94159934ce8","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

The research identified the F-shaped reading pattern in 2006, and a follow-up eyetracking study 11 years later confirmed these findings.\n\n## F-Pattern Components\n\nThe dominant reading pattern consists of three components: users first read in a horizontal movement, usually across the upper part of the content area, forming the F's top bar. Next, users move down the page a bit and then read across in a second horizontal movement that typically covers a shorter area, forming the F's lower bar. Finally, users scan the content's left side in a vertical movement.\n\n## Key Findings\n\nUsers' main reading behavior was fairly consistent across many different sites and tasks. The F viewing pattern is a rough, general shape rather than a uniform, pixel-perfect behavior.\n\nRecent eyetracking research shows that the F-shaped scanning pattern is alive and well in today's world — both on desktop and on mobile. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Eye-Tracking Reading Patterns\n\nEye-tracking research shows that there are 4 main patterns that people use to scan textual information on webpages: F-pattern, spotted pattern, layer-cake pattern, and commitment pattern.\n\n### Layer-Cake Pattern\nThe layer-cake scanning pattern consists of fixations made mostly on the page's headings and subheadings, with deliberate occasional fixations on the (body) text in between. In an eyetracking heatmap or gaze plot, this pattern looks roughly like a set of horizontal stripes and blank spaces between them, resembling a layer cake. Aside from reading every (or almost every) word, the layer-cake pattern is by far the most effective way to scan pages.\n\n### Spotted Pattern\nSpotted pattern consists of skipping big chunks of text and scanning as if looking for something specific, such as a link, digits, a particular word or a set of words with a distinctive shape (such as an address or signature).\n\n### Z-Pattern\nA Z-pattern is a reading pattern in which the eye moves in a Z-shape: from top to bottom, left to right, then diagonally down, left to right, and then horizontally. It is often found on simple landing pages.\n\nThese patterns represent how users naturally scan web content based on their goals and how the content is visually organized.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"308ea7fd-6ed2-4f9e-997d-4ea2264f2adc","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:03:23.049Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"95934901-808f-4396-8d3d-9fbe7c104798","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Here's a summary of the key findings:\n\n## Cognitive Load Theory Overview\n\nCognitive Load Theory (CLT) was coined in 1988 by John Sweller and suggests that our working memory is only able to hold a small amount of information at any one time and that instructional methods should avoid overloading it in order to maximize learning.\n\n## Text Formatting and Reading\n\nThe research reveals important connections between text formatting and cognitive load:\n\nMaking changes to text format (font, size, character width or spacing, weight, and line spacing) to improve readability may reduce the extraneous load on a reader's cognitive processing, freeing up more space in working memory for decoding or word recognition.\n\nResearch studies have shown a direct connection between reading fluency and comprehension, with individuals who process and recognize words quickly able to determine meaning and engage in critical thinking faster and more effectively than their peers.\n\n## Key Research Areas\n\nSweller originally suggested that the total cognitive load is the sum of intrinsic, extraneous, and germane loads when a learner is engaged in learning, with the overarching goal of CLT being to lessen the amount of extraneous load by changing the way instructions are presented.\n\nThe 'redundancy effect' occurs when learners are presented with additional information that is not directly relevant to learning, or with the same information in multiple forms, such as a textbook with both text and a diagram that repeat information.\n\nThe search results include several seminal works by Sweller and collaborators on cognitive load theory and instructional design, particularly regarding the format of instruction and its effects on learning.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the Miller of Harvard University's Department of Psychology. The average person can only keep 7 (plus or minus 2) items in their working memory.\n\n## Working Memory Capacity\n\nThe short-term memory, also called working memory, can store only about 5 to 9 pieces, 5 when the information is complex, and 9 when it is simple. However, later research on short-term memory and working memory revealed that memory span is not constant even when measured in chunks, and the number of chunks a human can recall depends on the category used.\n\n## Chunking Concept\n\nA chunk is the largest meaningful unit in the presented material that the person recognizes—thus, what counts as a chunk depends on the knowledge of the person being tested. The concept of chunks rather than the limit of about 7 items may be the most important specific contribution of the article.\n\n## Text Structure and Design Applications\n\nBreaking text into paragraphs applies Miller's Law, as each paragraph serves as a 'chunk' of related ideas, making it easier for the reader to process and understand the content. Organizing content into smaller chunks helps users process, understand, and memorize easily.\n\n## Important Caveats\n\nMiller's Law is commonly misunderstood, with designers frequently citing it to justify limiting navigation items to seven, though attributing such design dogma to Miller's Law is misleading and inaccurate.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ec0fc637-144e-4aba-aac1-904c20648f23","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:04:33.203Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"ad619848-3aa6-4ec3-ab7f-0c47a2ba0627","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Comprehension and Readability Benefits\n\nResearch in cognitive psychology shows that structured information reduces the mental effort required for comprehension. When information is presented in bullet format, our working memory can process it more efficiently because each point operates as a discrete unit.\n\nReaders scan content in 15 seconds, favoring scannable formats like bullet points for quick comprehension. Additionally, the 7±2 rule (also known as Miller's Law) explains why bullet points work so well: our working memory can comfortably hold 5-9 items at once, and well-crafted bullet lists respect this limitation by chunking information into digestible pieces.\n\n## Prose vs. Bullet Points\n\nResearch findings show mixed results depending on context. One study found that a brochure with illustrations and narrative text was given a significantly higher overall rating than one with bullet-type text and no illustrations, while among poor readers, comprehension was significantly greater for those who read the brochure with illustrations and narrative text.\n\n## Numbered vs. Unnumbered Lists\n\nNumbered lists are ideal when the order of items is critical, such as step-by-step procedures or ranking of importance, and they help readers follow a specific sequence and understand the hierarchy of the points being made. Here's a summary of the findings:\n\n## Overview of Bullet Characters and Symbols\n\nA bullet point, •, is a typographical mark used to introduce items in a list. The bullet symbol may take any of a variety of shapes, such as circular, square, diamond or arrow.\n\n## Types of List Markers\n\nThe search results identify several categories of symbols:\n\n- **Circular/Dots**: Classic round bullets, the most common choice\n- **Arrows**: Sequential bullets indicating progression or steps\n- **Dashes**: * (asterisk), - (hyphen), . Most accessibility experts suggest starting at 16pt as your baseline—and that's before users start adjusting their device settings.\n\n## Research on Mobile Reading\n\nStudies have shown that users read text on mobile screens about 25% slower than on desktop computers. The optimal reading distance for mobile devices is typically 20-24 inches from your face. At this distance, your eye can comfortably distinguish individual letters when they're at least 16 pixels tall—which is why that's become the minimum standard for body text.\n\n## WCAG Accessibility Standards\n\nThe WCAG recommendation is 4.5:1 for normal text up to 24px, 3:1 for bold text of at least 19px or larger, and 3:1 for regular text of at least 24px or larger. Additionally, when defining font sizes using CSS, the best practice would be to use relative units instead of absolute units. By leveraging relative units, you can make the text size on your website adaptable to different devices and user settings. The amount of gas in the tank will vary, depending on each user's inherent motivation and interest in each page's specific topic. The content above the fold received most attention (57% of viewing time); the second screenful of content received about a third of that (17% viewing time); the remaining 26% was spread in a long-tail distribution.\n\n## Scanning Patterns\n\nThe F-shaped pattern is the most common: This scanning pattern resembles the shape of the letter F, but it is rarely a perfect F. Other patterns include:\n\n- Layer-cake pattern occurs when the eyes scan headings and subheadings and skip the normal text below.\n- Spotted pattern consists of skipping big chunks of text and scanning as if looking for something specific.\n- Marking pattern involves keeping the eyes focused in one place as the mouse scrolls or finger swipes the page, and marking happens more on mobile than on desktop.\n\n## Mobile Considerations\n\nThe increase in the use of mobile devices has further changed reading habits, with smaller screens and vertical scrolling navigation reducing the effectiveness of the F-pattern in mobile interfaces, making models such as the Z-pattern more predominant.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"e813d7aa-1cbf-4589-ade6-d0613fc5283f","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:06:13.957Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"cba0d06b-c507-40d8-a24a-2676339ba53e","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

instructions or introducing ideas that will be discussed in a certain order\n- You want to display items in a numbered series\n- Numbers are easy to track and cite, making them useful for documents that need clear references\n\n**Bulleted lists** should be used when:\n- You want to draw visual attention to items in a list without implying that items go in a certain order (such as chronology, importance, or priority)\n- Order does not matter, and they are useful for listing related points, characteristics, or examples without implying any particular sequence\n\n## Research on here's what I found about the Dyson and Haselgrove study on line length, reading speed, and comprehension:\n\n## Study Overview\n\nDyson and Haselgrove investigated the effects of two reading speeds (normal and fast) and different line lengths on comprehension, reading rate and scrolling patterns when reading text on screen.\n\n## Key Findings\n\n**Line Length and Comprehension:**\nA medium line length (55 characters per line) appears to support effective reading at normal and fast speeds, and this produced the highest level of comprehension and was also read faster than short lines.\n\n**Reading Speed Effects:**\nA reduction in overall comprehension was found when reading fast, but the type of information recalled was not dependent on speed.\n\n**Scrolling Patterns:**\nScrolling patterns associated with better comprehension (more time in pauses and more individual scrolling movements) contrast with scrolling patterns used by faster readers (less time in pauses between scrolling).\n\n## Comprehension Assessment\n\nDyson and Haselgrove used a more comprehensive set of questions that required the reader to use higher order thinking, to remember order of items (structure), to recall main ideas, to recall incidental facts from the passage, and to indicate if a line was in the passage or not.\n\nThe study was published in 2001 in the International Here's what the research shows:\n\n## Chunking Theory Overview\n\nIn cognitive psychology, chunking is a process by which small individual pieces of a set of information are bound together to create a meaningful whole later on in memory. The chunks, by which the information is grouped, are meant to improve short-term retention of the material, thus bypassing the limited capacity of working memory and allowing the working memory to be more efficient.\n\n## Historical Foundation\n\nThe word chunking comes from a famous 1956 paper by George A. Miller, \"The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two: Some Limits on Our Capacity for Processing Information\". A variety of studies could be summarized by saying that short-term memory had a capacity of about \"seven plus-or-minus two\" chunks.\n\n## Cowan's Work on Working Memory\n\nNelson Cowan has made significant contributions to understanding chunking and working memory capacity. A series of studies by Cowan and colleagues have directly addressed whether memory capacity is limited by the number of items that can be held in memory or by the number of chunks. Cowan's research suggests working memory capacity ranges from 4±1 units at a time.\n\n## Effects on Comprehension and Retention\n\nChunking allows more items to be encoded into working memory with more available to transfer into long-term memory. Chunking is primarily focused on aiding comprehension and memory. The primary goal of chunking is to improve memory recall and comprehension, making it useful in a variety of contexts like learning, decision-making, and user experience design.\n\n## Paragraph Breaks and Text Structure\n\nWhile not explicitly detailed in the search results regarding paragraph breaks specifically, the key to effectively chunking multimedia content (text as well as images, graphics, videos, buttons, and other elements) is to keep related things close together and aligned. Using background colors, horizontal rules, and white space can help users visually distinguish between what's related and what isn't.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"91dd3c3b-062a-420b-b930-74e2e409e992","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:08:13.701Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"6fca4688-f3e4-4f7f-9976-986e6c7b078a","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Ling and van Schaik Web Readability Studies\n\n### Study 1: Font Type and Line Length (2006)\n\nLing and van Schaik (2006) carried out two experiments to examine the influence of typeface and line length on students' use of web pages, presenting web pages in either a serif typeface (12-point Times New Roman) or a sans serif typeface (10-point Arial) in four different line lengths.\n\nKey findings:\n- The results show a user preference for Arial, the sans-serif font used in the experiment, over Times New Roman, the selected serif typeface.\n- There was little impact of font on task performance, although the effect of line length was significant, with longer line lengths facilitating better scanning and shorter line lengths leading to better subjective outcomes.\n\n### Study 2: Line Spacing and Text Alignment (2007)\n\nText presented in the content area appeared in one of three line spacings: single-, 1.5- or double spacing, either left-aligned or justified, with the font used being Arial 10 point.\n\nKey findings:\n- Double spacing significantly enhances visual search speed and accuracy compared to single or 1.5 spacing.\n- Participants performed better with left-aligned text than justified, achieving greater accuracy and speed in visual search tasks.\n\nThese experiments focused on visual search and information retrieval performance on web pages, measuring both objective performance metrics and subjective user preferences.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"d055aa5d-2e83-4c0d-9d05-9a19ad76760f","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:08:44.521Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"7527a2f2-e706-4ad9-bffd-e798be51af9e","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Success Criterion 1.4.12 Overview\n\nWCAG 1.4.12 Text Spacing is a crucial success criterion introduced in WCAG 2.1, requiring that users can adjust text spacing to specific metrics without any loss of content or functionality.\n\n## Required Text Spacing Metrics\n\nThe criterion specifies line height (line spacing) to at least 1.5 times the font size; spacing following paragraphs to at least 2 times the font size; letter spacing (tracking) to at least 0.12 times the font size; and word spacing to at least 0.16 times the font size.\n\n## User Benefits\n\nThe intent of this Success Criterion is to help ensure that people with disabilities who override spacing can read text. For example, people with low vision or dyslexia may override an author's set spacing to enable readability or increased reading speed.\n\nThe ability to customize text spacing is vital for users with low vision, as many individuals with low vision find that increasing the space between characters, words, and lines significantly improves the readability of text.\n\n## Key Implementation Requirements\n\nThe requirement is to ensure that when a user overrides the authored text spacing, content or functionality is not lost. The core idea is to prevent web content from clipping, overlapping, or becoming unreadable when a user's custom stylesheet or browser extension applies significantly increased spacing. Websites must be designed flexibly enough to accommodate these user-driven adjustments.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"14284952-8c5b-4845-83c4-338749529374","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:08:54.515Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"03adb7c7-dc48-4aa8-b1ba-d976f317fa53","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Most people don't read web pages; they scan them, glancing over pages looking for keywords, links, and headings that seem relevant to their goal.\n\n## Core User Behavior Principles\n\nKrug identifies three key facts about how users actually behave on the web:\n\n1. **Scanning, not reading**: People scan; they don't read.\n\n2. **Satisficing**: People choose the first reasonable option, rather than seeking the optimal choice.\n\n3. **Muddling through**: People muddle through things rather than figure them out.\n\n## Design Implications for Scanning\n\nSince users scan rather than read, use familiar layouts (logo links home, menus on top/left), clear visual hierarchies, headings, bullets, and short paragraphs to make content easy to find at a glance. Additionally, by catering to this scanning behavior, you reduce cognitive load and help users find what they need faster.\n\nThis understanding of scanning behavior forms the foundation of Krug's usability principles and is central to designing websites that users can navigate intuitively without requiring deep thought.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"9cc9c1df-2321-4276-ad0c-59e950ba4992","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:09:05.789Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"d817be64-6b52-4846-b853-36afd1513f1f","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

than for light text on dark background (negative polarity).\n\n### Study on Younger and Older Adults\n\nThe researchers investigated the effect of display polarity on visual acuity and proofreading performance for younger and older adults, and found that visual acuity testing and a proofreading task revealed a positive polarity advantage for both groups, with dark characters on light background leading to better legibility.\n\n### Mechanism: Pupil Size and Proofreading\n\nThe researchers investigated the underlying mechanism by assessing pupil size and proofreading performance when reading positive and negative polarity texts, testing the display luminance hypothesis which postulates that the typically greater brightness of positive compared to negative polarity displays leads to smaller pupil sizes and, hence, a sharper retinal image and better perception of detail.\n\n### Character Size Effects\n\nOne of their studies was titled \"Positive Display Polarity Is Particularly Advantageous for Small Character Sizes,\" and their results showed that light mode won across all dimensions, with the positive contrast polarity being better for both visual-acuity tasks and proofreading tasks.\n\nThese studies provide empirical evidence supporting light mode (positive polarity) over dark mode (negative polarity) for reading and proofreading tasks.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST VSTF breaks sentences up at salient clause and phrase boundaries, fits each row of text into one or two fixation eyespans, uses a cascading pattern to denote syntactic hierarchies, and creates visual clusters across multiple rows to help readers retain and integrate multi-phrase images.\n\n## Research Findings on Reading Comprehension\n\nThe research on VSTF's effectiveness for reading comprehension is substantial:\n\n**For High School Students:** In each grade, VSTF students had significantly higher scores on nationally standardized (and conventionally formatted) reading proficiency tests over controls; effect sizes ranged from .41 to .69 standard deviations, and the one-year growth in reading proficiency with VSTF was equivalent to 2 to 3 years' of additional growth in study and national controls.\n\n**For College Readers:** Among college readers, the VSTF method instantly increased reading comprehension and efficiency of reading online text, while reducing eyestrain.\n\n**For EFL Learners:** Reformatted texts could support EFL students to enhance their ERF elements.\n\nNote: While the search results reference work by researchers like Stan Walker, Phil Schloss, Charles Fletcher, and Charles Vogel, I did not find specific information about Wichary Kasprzyk in the search results.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"d7a0c083-6859-4fe8-9ef9-1b3147933cc2","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:09:51.050Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"7d898101-1efc-4bca-8912-426ae93c4de1","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Cowan argued that there is a more precise capacity limit of only three to five chunks, and brought together a wide variety of data on capacity limits suggesting that this smaller capacity limit is real.\n\n## Key Findings\n\nA single, central capacity limit averaging about four chunks is implicated along with other, noncapacity-limited sources. A capacity limit for the focus of attention is proposed.\n\n## Boundary Conditions\n\nFour basic conditions in which chunks can be identified and capacity limits can accordingly be observed are: (1) when information overload limits chunks to individual stimulus items, (2) when other steps are taken specifically to block the recoding of stimulus items into larger chunks, (3) in performance discontinuities caused by the capacity limit, and (4) in various indirect effects of the capacity limit.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"ae6c6778-ce2e-482e-90bb-82312e923591","timestamp":"2026-03-18T15:10:15.370Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"0c59b6ae-4fc2-4560-9dab-892044ce9500","userType":"external","entrypoint":"claude-vscode","cwd":"/Users/mini/.openclaw/workspace/github","sessionId":"4932b309-e6b7-40e8-bd10-ca9e425fd4f8","version":"2.1.78","gitBranch":"main","slug":"wobbly-exploring-locket"}

Let me write it up.\n\n---\n\n# The Neuroscience and Cognitive Science of Reading Text on Screens (Especially Mobile)\n\n## 1. EYE MOVEMENT PATTERNS ON SCREENS\n\n### Saccades and Fixation Points\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Saccades are rapid eye movements that bring different parts of text onto the fovea (the most sensitive part of the retina). Fixations are the periods between saccades when the eye pauses to uptake visual information. The average saccade during reading covers 7-9 letter spaces, though this varies by font size and type. Fixations during reading typically last 200-250ms.\n- Source: [Royal Society Open Science - Fixations and Saccades Survey](https://royalsocietypublishing.org/doi/10.1098/rsos.180502)\n- Source: [PMC - Using Eye Movements to Evaluate Cognitive Processes in Text Comprehension](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4089416/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** On mobile devices, eye-tracking is more challenging because scrolling creates dynamic stimuli. A fixation on a scrolling mobile feed is defined as >60ms of consecutive gaze points within an area of interest. Detection of fixations and saccades for dynamic stimuli (scrolling feeds) is barely better than chance with traditional algorithms. A 120 Hz mobile eye tracker significantly improves saccade detection over 60 Hz.\n- Source: [PMC - Sampling Rate Influences Saccade Detection in Mobile Eye Tracking](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7141092/)\n- Source: [Frontiers - Optimizing Fixation Filters for Eye-Tracking on Small Screens](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnins.2021.578439/full)\n\n### The F-Pattern (Nielsen Norman Group)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** NNG recorded how 232 users looked at thousands of web pages. The F-shaped reading pattern was identified in 2006 and confirmed in a follow-up study 11 years later. The pattern has three components:\n1. Users first read horizontally across the upper content area (top bar of the F)\n2. Users move down and read a shorter horizontal area (lower bar of the F)\n3. Users scan the left side vertically (stem of the F)\n\nThe F-pattern is a rough, general shape rather than a pixel-perfect behavior. It is still relevant on mobile.\n- Source: [NNG - F-Shaped Pattern Original Study](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/f-shaped-pattern-reading-web-content-discovered/)\n- Source: [NNG - F-Shaped Pattern Still Relevant Even on Mobile](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/f-shaped-pattern-reading-web-content/)\n\n### Other Patterns\n\n**[VERIFIED]** NNG's eye-tracking research identified 4 main scanning patterns:\n- **Layer-cake pattern**: Fixations mostly on headings and subheadings, skipping body text between. Looks like horizontal stripes with blanks between them. Aside from reading every word, this is the most effective scanning pattern.\n- **Spotted pattern**: Skipping large chunks of text, scanning for something specific (links, digits, a particular word, addresses, signatures).\n- **Commitment pattern**: Reading nearly every word on the page.\n- **Z-pattern**: Eye moves in a Z-shape; more common on simple landing pages with minimal text.\n- Source: [NNG - Text Scanning Patterns: Eyetracking Evidence](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/text-scanning-patterns-eyetracking/)\n- Source: [NNG - The Layer-Cake Pattern of Scanning Content on the Web](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/layer-cake-pattern-scanning/)\n\n### Mobile Scrolling and Reading Patterns\n\n**[VERIFIED]** NNG found that content above the fold receives 57% of viewing time. The second screenful receives about 17%. The remaining 26% is distributed in a long tail. Users arrive at a page with a certain amount of \"fuel\" and consume it as they scroll down.\n- Source: [NNG - Scrolling and Attention Original Research](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/scrolling-and-attention-original-research/)\n- Source: [NNG - Scrolling and Attention](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/scrolling-and-attention/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** On mobile, a \"marking pattern\" occurs more than on desktop -- users keep their eyes focused in one place as the finger swipes the page. The smaller screen and vertical scrolling reduce the effectiveness of the F-pattern, making the Z-pattern more predominant on mobile.\n- Source: [NNG - F-Shaped Pattern Reading Web Content](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/f-shaped-pattern-reading-web-content/)\n\n### Scanning vs. Reading\n\n**[VERIFIED]** 79% of web users scan rather than read; only 16% read word-by-word (Nielsen, 1997, reconfirmed in later studies). Reading from screens is about 25% slower than reading from paper. Users feel compelled to move and click rather than sit and read.\n- Source: [NNG - How Users Read on the Web](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/how-users-read-on-the-web/)\n- Source: [NNG - Why Web Users Scan Instead of Reading](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/why-web-users-scan-instead-reading/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Steve Krug's three facts about web reading: (1) People scan, they don't read; (2) People satisfice -- they choose the first reasonable option, not the optimal one; (3) People muddle through rather than figure things out.\n- Source: [Don't Make Me Think, Revisited - Steve Krug](https://www.amazon.com/Dont-Make-Think-Revisited-Usability/dp/0321965515)\n\n---\n\n## 2. OPTIMAL LINE LENGTH FOR COMPREHENSION\n\n### The 50-75 CPL Standard\n\n**[VERIFIED]** A literature review by Nanavati and Bias (2005) in *Visible Language* analyzed the research on optimal line length. The recommendation is 50-75 characters per line (CPL), with 66 CPL often cited as the sweet spot.\n- Source: [Visible Language - Optimal Line Length in Reading: A Literature Review](https://journals.uc.edu/index.php/vl/article/view/5765)\n- Source: [ERIC - Optimal Line Length in Reading](https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ749012)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Dyson and Haselgrove (2001) found that a medium line length of 55 CPL supports effective reading at both normal and fast speeds, produces the highest level of comprehension, and is read faster than short lines.\n- Source: [ScienceDirect - The Influence of Reading Speed and Line Length](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1071581901904586)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Shaikh and Chaparro found that longer line lengths (55 CPL, 75-100 CPL, 100 CPL) resulted in faster online reading speeds. Longer lines are better for quick scanning; shorter lines are better for accuracy and thorough reading.\n- Source: [The Effect of Line Length and Passage Type on Reading Speed, Comprehension, and User Satisfaction](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/250841956_The_Effect_of_Line_Length_and_Passage_Type_on_Reading_Speed_Comprehension_and_User_Satisfaction)\n\n### Mobile Line Length\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** For mobile devices, the recommended line length is 30-50 characters per line, with some sources suggesting 35-45 CPL. The desktop recommendation of 60-75 CPL is roughly halved for mobile.\n- Source: [UXPin - Optimal Line Length for Readability](https://www.uxpin.com/studio/blog/optimal-line-length-for-readability/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** WCAG guideline 1.4.8 states that lines of text should not exceed 80 characters (40 for CJK text).\n- Source: [W3C - Understanding Success Criterion 1.4.12: Text Spacing](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/text-spacing.html)\n\n### Speed vs. Comprehension vs. Retention\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Both very short and very long lines slow down reading by interrupting normal eye-movement patterns. Lines too long cause readers to skim the left margin instead of fully engaging. Lines too short break reading rhythm. Interestingly, users prefer shorter lines (4-inch / ~40 CPL) even though longer lines are read faster -- a gap between subjective preference and measurable performance.\n- Source: [Baymard Institute - Readability: The Optimal Line Length](https://baymard.com/blog/line-length-readability)\n- Source: [Design Regression - Line Length Revisited: Following the Research](https://designregression.com/article/line-length-revisited-following-the-research)\n\n### LinkedIn / Social Media Specifics\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** On LinkedIn mobile, the \"see more\" cutoff appears after approximately 140 characters. More than 57% of LinkedIn traffic comes from mobile devices. Posts with 150-300 characters tend to get the most engagement. You have about 2 lines before content is truncated.\n- Source: [LinkedIn Pulse - LinkedIn \"See More\" Break Points](https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/linkedin-see-more-break-points-john-espirian)\n- Source: [Social Rails - LinkedIn Post Character Limits 2026](https://socialrails.com/blog/linkedin-post-character-limits)\n\n---\n\n## 3. LINE SPACING AND PARAGRAPH LENGTH\n\n### Optimal Line Spacing (Leading)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** WCAG 2.1 Success Criterion 1.4.12 requires line height of at least 1.5x the font size, spacing following paragraphs at least 2x the font size, letter spacing at least 0.12x font size, and word spacing at least 0.16x font size.\n- Source: [W3C - Understanding Success Criterion 1.4.12: Text Spacing](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/text-spacing.html)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Beymer, Russell, and Orton (2008) tested line spacings from 0.8 to 1.8 (Arial font) with 104 participants on Wikipedia. They found marginal effects for line spacing, but the two extremes (0.8 and 1.8) impaired readability. Default line spacing (~1.2) worked well.\n- Source: [ACM - Make It Big!: The Effect of Font Size and Line Spacing on Online Readability](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2858036.2858204)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Ling and van Schaik (2007) tested single, 1.5, and double spacing with Arial 10pt. Double spacing significantly enhanced visual search speed and accuracy compared to single or 1.5 spacing.\n- Source: [ScienceDirect - The Influence of Line Spacing and Text Alignment on Visual Search](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0141938207000133)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Rello and Baeza-Yates tested line spacings of 0.8, 1.0, 1.4, and 1.8 with people with and without dyslexia. They found that font size has a significant effect on readability, while line spacing does not (at least for the sizes tested). They recommended 18pt font size.\n- Source: [Semantic Scholar - Size Matters (Spacing Not): 18 Points for a Dyslexic-Friendly Wikipedia](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Size-matters-(spacing-not):-18-points-for-a-Rello-Pielot/5608197a620c2151cd13d9db65526bfbd3299eb2)\n\n### Summary of Line Spacing Research\n\n| Spacing | Findings |\n|---------|----------|\n| 1.0 (single) | Adequate but not optimal for screen reading |\n| 1.15-1.2 | Common default; generally acceptable |\n| 1.5 | WCAG minimum; reduces eye strain; recommended for body text |\n| 2.0 (double) | Best for visual search tasks (Ling & van Schaik); may break text unity if excessive |\n\n### Paragraph Length\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** Optimal paragraph length for web content: 200-500 characters including spaces, or approximately 2-4 sentences. Average sentence length should be 15-20 words. 8-12 words per line creates the most comfortable reading experience.\n- Source: [Adoc Studio - Typography Best Practices 2026](https://www.adoc-studio.app/blog/typography-guide)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Properly using whitespace between paragraphs and in margins can increase comprehension up to 20%, but too much whitespace between lines can break unity and make the design feel disconnected.\n- Source: [ResearchGate - The Importance of Text Width and White Space for Online Documentation](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/37358612_The_importance_of_text_width_and_white_space_for_online_documentation)\n\n### Single-Sentence Paragraphs\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** Single-sentence paragraphs can provide \"extra punch\" and are common in newspapers (most paragraphs are 1-2 sentences). However, overuse diminishes the effect. The American Press Institute found readers understand 100% of content when sentences average 8 words; comprehension plummets below 10% for sentences averaging 43 words.\n- Source: [Copyblogger - The 2nd Most Important Element in Copywriting](https://copyblogger.com/first-sentence/)\n- Source: [Doris and Bertie - Word's Readability Score](https://www.dorisandbertie.com/goodcopybadcopy/2011/06/02/how-to-use-readability-score)\n\n---\n\n## 4. COGNITIVE LOAD THEORY AND TEXT FORMATTING\n\n### Cognitive Load Theory Basics\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Cognitive Load Theory (CLT), coined by John Sweller in 1988, posits that working memory can hold only a small amount of information at any one time. Three types of load: intrinsic (complexity of the material), extraneous (how the material is presented), and germane (effort devoted to learning). The goal is to minimize extraneous load.\n- Source: [Sweller, 1988 - Cognitive Load During Problem Solving: Effects on Learning](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1207/s15516709cog1202_4)\n- Source: [Readability Matters - Increase Readability, Reduce Cognitive Load](https://readabilitymatters.org/articles/increase-readability-reduce-cognitive-load)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Making changes to text format (font, size, character width, spacing, weight, line spacing) to improve readability may reduce extraneous load on cognitive processing, freeing working memory for decoding, word recognition, and comprehension.\n- Source: [Readability Matters - Increase Readability, Reduce Cognitive Load](https://readabilitymatters.org/articles/increase-readability-reduce-cognitive-load)\n\n### Visual Noise\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Visual noise in text comes from insufficient whitespace, clutter, ALL CAPS, excessive italics, underlining, and inconsistent formatting. Sufficient whitespace prevents visual fatigue. Short paragraphs separated by empty lines support chunking and reduce cognitive overload. Bold should be used for emphasis instead of italics, ALL CAPS, or underlining.\n- Source: [Mayer and Moreno - 9 Ways to Reduce Cognitive Load (PDF)](https://www.uky.edu/~gmswan3/544/9_ways_to_reduce_CL.pdf)\n- Source: [NNG - 4 Principles to Reduce Cognitive Load in Forms](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/4-principles-reduce-cognitive-load/)\n\n### Miller's Law (7 plus/minus 2)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** George Miller (1956) published \"The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two.\" The key contribution was the concept of chunks, not the number 7 itself. What counts as a \"chunk\" depends on the knowledge of the person being tested. The law is commonly misunderstood -- attributing design rules like \"limit navigation to 7 items\" to Miller is misleading.\n- Source: [Miller, 1956 - The Magical Number Seven (PDF)](https://labs.la.utexas.edu/gilden/files/2016/04/MagicNumberSeven-Miller1956.pdf)\n- Source: [Laws of UX - Miller's Law](https://lawsofux.com/millers-law/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Cowan (2001) refined Miller's estimate, arguing the true capacity limit of focused attention is about 3-5 chunks, averaging 4. This supersedes the 7 plus/minus 2 estimate.\n- Source: [Cowan, 2001 - The Magical Number 4 in Short-Term Memory](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11515286/)\n- Source: [PMC - The Magical Mystery Four: How is Working Memory Capacity Limited?](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2864034/)\n\n### Chunking and Paragraph Breaks\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Chunking is the process of binding small individual pieces of information into meaningful wholes, allowing more items to be held in working memory. Each paragraph can serve as a \"chunk\" of related ideas, making it easier for readers to process and understand content. The key to effective chunking: keep related things close together and aligned; use background colors, horizontal rules, and whitespace to distinguish what is related from what is not.\n- Source: [NNG - How Chunking Helps Content Processing](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/chunking/)\n- Source: [Wikipedia - Chunking (psychology)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chunking_(psychology))\n\n### What Happens with TOO MUCH Whitespace\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Research on fragmented reading shows significant cognitive consequences. When text is excessively broken up:\n- Decreased reading comprehension occurs\n- Users' attention quickly switches between fragments without in-depth thinking\n- Fragmentation encourages a \"somewhat mindless approach to texts\"\n- It negatively impacts deep information-processing capacity and ability to sustain attention\n- It can cause \"inertia and atomized thinking\"\n- It hurts cognitive abilities in higher stages including conceptual cognition and maintenance of working memory representations\n- Source: [PMC - Effects of University Students' Fragmented Reading on Cognitive Development](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9415515/)\n- Source: [Frontiers - Attention Switching Through Text Dissimilarity](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/human-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnhum.2024.1402746/full)\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** Medium levels of whitespace produce higher satisfaction than either very solid or very spread-out text. The optimal balance is moderate, purposeful whitespace.\n- Source: [ResearchGate - The Importance of Text Width and White Space](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/37358612_The_importance_of_text_width_and_white_space_for_online_documentation)\n\n---\n\n## 5. BULLET POINTS AND LISTS\n\n### Bullets vs. Prose\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Participants who saw items presented as a bulleted list remembered 33.4% more items than those who saw the same items in paragraph form. 34.5% of participants recalled all three items in the bulleted list condition vs. 22.7% in the paragraph condition.\n- Source: [Lyngo Lab - Bulleted Lists Improve Memory](https://www.lyngolab.com/bulleted-lists-memory.html)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Lorch's research on text signaling devices (headings, overviews, summaries) found that signals produced recalls that were better organized by text topics, and subjects remembered more topics (but recalled less about each topic) when text contained signals.\n- Source: [ResearchGate - How Bulleted Lists and Enumerations in Formatted Paragraphs Affect Recall](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/290470586_How_bulleted_lists_and_enumerations_in_formatted_paragraphs_affect_recall_and_evaluation_of_functional_ext)\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** However, one study found that a brochure with illustrations and narrative text was rated significantly higher overall than one with bullet-type text and no illustrations. Among poor readers, comprehension was significantly greater for narrative text with illustrations.\n- Source: [Yoast - The Psychology of Scannable Content and Bullet Points](https://yoast.com/the-psychology-of-scannable-content-and-bullet-points/)\n\n### Numbered Lists vs. Bullets\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Use numbered lists when order matters (step-by-step procedures, chronological order, ranking of importance). Use unnumbered/bulleted lists when order does not matter (listing related points, characteristics, examples without implying sequence). Participants using numbered-list websites visited more pages and rated the site higher than those using paragraph-formatted sites.\n- Source: [APA Style - Bulleted Lists](https://apastyle.apa.org/style-grammar-guidelines/lists/bulleted)\n- Source: [Enago Academy - How to Use Bullet Lists and Number Lists in a Research Paper](https://www.enago.com/academy/using-formatting-lists-academic-writing/)\n\n### Bullet Character Types\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** No peer-reviewed research directly comparing bullet characters (dot, dash, arrow, etc.) for readability was found. The solid bullet (bullet) is the conventional default. Dashes (-) perform the same function but do not stand out as well. Sans-serif fonts work best with bullets for maximum legibility. Bullets should be optically centered on neighboring text.\n- Source: [Wikipedia - Bullet (typography)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullet_(typography))\n\n### How Many Items Before Overwhelming\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Based on working memory research (Miller 1956, Cowan 2001), lists should respect the capacity limit of approximately 4-7 chunks. Well-crafted bullet lists respect this limitation by chunking information into discrete units. Lists of 5-9 items are manageable; going beyond 9 risks exceeding working memory capacity.\n- Source: [Laws of UX - Miller's Law](https://lawsofux.com/millers-law/)\n- Source: [Cowan, 2001 - The Magical Number 4](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11515286/)\n- Source: [Yoast - The Psychology of Scannable Content](https://yoast.com/the-psychology-of-scannable-content-and-bullet-points/)\n\n### Nested Lists\n\n**[UNVERIFIED]** Nested lists help display secondary/child entity information within a larger context. Their success hinges on how well the structure mirrors the user's mental model. If logical and predictable, it enhances usability; if confusing, it detracts. No specific peer-reviewed research was found on the cognitive impact of nesting depth, but the general principle of reducing cognitive load applies -- each nesting level adds complexity.\n- Source: [Oreate AI Blog - Unpacking Nested Lists in UX Design](https://www.oreateai.com/blog/beyond-the-surface-unpacking-nested-lists-in-ux-design/9eafd3cb4d7b5250872ceaa0140deaaf)\n\n---\n\n## 6. TYPOGRAPHY ON MOBILE SCREENS\n\n### Sans-Serif vs. Serif on Screens\n\n**[VERIFIED]** A 2022 study (ScienceDirect) found that the presence or absence of serifs may be less important than stroke contrast. Low stroke contrast reduces legibility thresholds more than serif presence. The notion that serifs are harder to read on modern high-resolution screens has been debunked -- it was based on the era of low-resolution displays.\n- Source: [ScienceDirect - The Dispute About Sans Serif Versus Serif Fonts: An Interaction Between Variables](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S000169182200138X)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** An eye-tracking study found Verdana (sans-serif) performed best, with faster reading and fewer regressions. For people with low vision, sans-serif fonts are more readable. However, overall there was no significant difference between serif and sans-serif for screen or print display.\n- Source: [ResearchGate - Keeping Your Readers' Eyes on the Screen](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/232915903_Keeping_Your_Readers'_Eyes_on_the_Screen_An_Eye-Tracking_Study_Comparing_Sans_Serif_and_Serif_Typefaces)\n- Source: [PMC - Serifs and Font Legibility](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4612630/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Rello and Baeza-Yates (2013) tested 12 fonts with 97 subjects: sans serif, monospaced, and roman font styles significantly improved reading performance over serif, proportional, and italic fonts.\n- Source: [ACM - Good Fonts for Dyslexia](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2513383.2513447)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** A 2024 PMC study concluded: \"There is no 'one size fits all' solution\" -- font type selection should consider line spacing, individual user needs, and task type.\n- Source: [PMC - Font Matters: Impact of Font Types on Attention and Working Memory](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11156575/)\n\n### Optimal Font Size for Mobile\n\n**[VERIFIED]** 16px is the practical minimum for body text on mobile phones. At the typical mobile reading distance (20-24 inches), the eye can comfortably distinguish letters at 16px or larger. Users read on mobile about 25% slower than on desktop. Beymer et al. found that font sizes 18 and 26 produced significantly more correct comprehension responses.\n- Source: [The A11Y Collective - WCAG Minimum Font Size](https://www.a11y-collective.com/blog/wcag-minimum-font-size/)\n- Source: [Smashing Magazine - Reference Guide for Typography in Mobile Web Design](https://www.smashingmagazine.com/2018/06/reference-guide-typography-mobile-web-design/)\n- Source: [ACM - Make It Big!](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2858036.2858204)\n\n### Contrast Ratios\n\n**[VERIFIED]** WCAG 2.0 Level AA requires a contrast ratio of at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text (18pt+ regular or 14pt+ bold). The 4.5:1 threshold was chosen based on research showing that users with 20/40 vision require approximately this level of contrast. The ratio measures relative luminance, not hue.\n- Source: [W3C - Understanding Success Criterion 1.4.3: Contrast (Minimum)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/contrast-minimum.html)\n- Source: [WebAIM - Contrast and Color Accessibility](https://webaim.org/articles/contrast/)\n\n### Dark Mode vs. Light Mode\n\n**[VERIFIED]** Piepenbrock, Mayr, and Buchner found the \"positive polarity advantage\": dark text on light background (light mode) leads to better visual acuity and proofreading performance for both younger and older adults. The mechanism: lighter backgrounds cause smaller pupil sizes, leading to sharper retinal images and better detail perception. This advantage is particularly strong for small character sizes.\n- Source: [PubMed - Smaller Pupil Size and Better Proofreading with Positive Polarity](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25135324/)\n- Source: [PubMed - Positive Display Polarity Is Advantageous for Both Younger and Older Adults](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23654206/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** NNG's review: In people with normal vision, visual performance tends to be better with light mode. For long-form reading, light mode tends to be easier for comprehension. Dark mode is more suitable for tasks requiring less visual acuity (browsing social media). Some people with cataracts may perform better with dark mode. Dark mode is objectively worse for people with astigmatism.\n- Source: [NNG - Dark Mode vs. Light Mode: Which Is Better?](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/dark-mode/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** A study in *Scientific Reports* found sustained exposure to light mode may be associated with myopia (choroid thinning), while dark mode reading caused choroid thickening. This is a counterpoint to the general light-mode advantage.\n- Source: [NNG - Dark Mode vs. Light Mode](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/dark-mode/)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** A 2025 PMC study on tablet users found dark mode may help reduce the risk of eye fatigue compared to light mode, but light mode was better for tasks requiring high visual acuity.\n- Source: [PMC - Immediate Effects of Light Mode and Dark Mode on Visual Fatigue in Tablet Users](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12027292/)\n\n---\n\n## BONUS: Visual-Syntactic Text Formatting (VSTF)\n\n**[VERIFIED]** VSTF algorithms break sentences at clause/phrase boundaries, fit each row into 1-2 fixation eyespans, and use cascading patterns to denote syntactic hierarchies. In high school students, VSTF produced significantly higher reading proficiency scores with effect sizes of 0.41-0.69 standard deviations; one year of VSTF equaled 2-3 years of additional reading growth. Among college readers, VSTF instantly increased comprehension and reading efficiency while reducing eyestrain.\n- Source: [IEEE Xplore - Visual-Syntactic Text Formatting: Theoretical Basis and Empirical Evidence](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/4464068/)\n- Source: [ResearchGate - Visual-Syntactic Text Formatting: A New Method to Enhance Online Reading](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/286342762_Visual-syntactic_text_formatting_A_new_method_to_enhance_online_reading)\n\n---\n\n## Summary of Key Numbers\n\n| Parameter | Optimal Value | Source Quality |\n|-----------|--------------|----------------|\n| Line length (desktop) | 50-75 CPL, sweet spot 55-66 | [VERIFIED] |\n| Line length (mobile) | 30-50 CPL | [UNVERIFIED] |\n| Line spacing | 1.5x font size (WCAG minimum) | [VERIFIED] |\n| Paragraph spacing | 2x font size (WCAG minimum) | [VERIFIED] |\n| Font size (mobile body) | 16px minimum, 18px+ preferred | [VERIFIED] |\n| Contrast ratio | 4.5:1 minimum (WCAG AA) | [VERIFIED] |\n| Working memory capacity | 3-5 chunks (avg 4, Cowan) or 5-9 (Miller) | [VERIFIED] |\n| List items before overwhelming | ~5-9 items | [VERIFIED] |\n| Scanning vs. reading | 79% scan, 16% read word-by-word | [VERIFIED] |\n| Above-fold attention | 57% of viewing time | [VERIFIED] |\n| Light vs. dark mode | Light mode wins for comprehension;
